Image Types and Formats

Raster

Raster images are made up of pixels. Each pixel contains color information, and together these pixels form an image. Raster images are mainly used for photographs. They usually come from cameras and scanners, but they can also be created using software. Any image with formats such as JPG/JPEG, PNG, GIF, etc., is essentially a raster image.

Because raster images are pixel-based, the larger the image size, the more pixels it contains, which results in a larger file size. On the web, we usually do not serve very large images because they take more time to download. At the same time, very small raster images are also not ideal, because when they are scaled up, they look blurry or pixelated due to insufficient pixel data.

Vector

In contrast, vector images are not made of pixels. Instead, they are defined using mathematical formulas (vectors) such as lines, curves, and shapes. Vector images are commonly used for logos, icons, and graphics. They are created using software like Adobe Illustrator, CorelDRAW, or other vector-based design tools.

Vector images are usually exported in SVG format, which stands for Scalable Vector Graphics. SVG images can be scaled to any size without losing quality and always remain sharp and clear, because they are based on mathematical definitions rather than pixels.

Raster VS Vector

Raster image formats: WebP is a modern image format that many developers are still not familiar with. WebP images are smaller in file size while maintaining good quality. However, WebP is not supported in Internet Explorer. Always use the Can I Use tool to check which browsers support WebP or any other CSS property.

JPEG, PNG, and WebP support 16 million colors, which allows us to include more detail in images. These formats are commonly used for high-quality images.

Transparency: JPEG does not support transparency, but PNG, GIF, and WebP do. This means we can download transparent images and place them on any background.

Animation: For long animations, video formats are recommended. However, GIF and WebP support animations. WebP can be used to create short, high-quality animations, while JPEG does not support animation at all.

Color limitations: GIF supports transparency and animation but is limited to 256 colors, which reduces image quality.

Usage:

  • Use JPEG for most photos and detailed images
  • Use PNG for transparent images
  • Use GIF for simple transparency and basic animations
  • Use WebP for smaller file sizes, transparency, and short animations (when browser support allows)

Content Images

In HTML, there are two main types of images: content images and background images.
Content images are part of the page’s information, while background images are used mainly for design and decoration.

We should always use the alt (alternative text) attribute to describe an image. The alt text is essential for blind users and people who use screen readers. Screen readers read the alt text aloud to explain what the image represents.

If an image is purely decorative and does not add meaningful content, we should use an empty alt attribute (alt=""). This tells screen readers to ignore the image. If we do not provide an alt attribute, screen readers may read the image file name, which is confusing and not helpful.

When using a JPEG image with a small resolution, such as 400 × 400 pixels, scaling it up to fill the entire screen will make it look blurry and pixelated. This happens because raster images lose quality when enlarged.

Although SVG images can scale perfectly without losing quality, they are not suitable for complex, photo-like images. SVGs are made using mathematical paths, lines, curves, and points, and creating detailed JPEG-style images in SVG format would be very time-consuming and inefficient. 

<img src="NCS.png" alt="NaseebCodeStudio Logo Image">

<img src="image_name.jpg" alt="A Random Food image in jpg format" class="img2">

If the Image is smaller it will look blurry
.img2{
      width: 100vw;
}

Background Images

Background images are used for decorative purposes, such as placing an image behind a box or any HTML element.
In CSS, the background-image property is used to set an image as the background of an element.

CSS also provides a shorthand property called background, which can be used to set the background image, background color, and other background-related properties in a single line.

Background images are mainly used for decoration, like adding an image behind a box or any element on a webpage.
The background-image property is used to apply a background image. However, CSS also offers a shorthand property called background, which allows us to set a background image or color using a single property. 

body{
      background: url(NCS.png);
}

Background Repeat

By default, a background image repeats itself to fill the entire container if the image is smaller. However, we have full control over the background image using CSS properties. 

body{
      background: url(NCS.png);

      background-repeat: repeat-x;
      background-repeat: repeat-y;
      background-repeat: repeat;
      background-repeat: no-repeat;
}

Background Position

By default, a background image is positioned at the top-left corner of its container. In most cases, this container is the body element. However, we can control the image position using CSS properties. 

body{
      background: url(NCS.png);
      background-repeat: no-repeat;

      Horizontal-Value Vertical-Value
      background-position: 100px 100px;
      background-position: -100px 100px;
      background-position: 100% 100px;
      background-position: center;
}

Background Size

To control the size of a background image, we use the background-size property.

When the container has a height larger than the viewport for example, setting the body height to 300vh the page becomes scrollable. If background-size: cover is used, the image will scale to cover the entire container while maintaining its aspect ratio. This can sometimes cause unexpected results during scrolling because the image stretches to fill the full height of the element. 

body{
      background: url(NCS.png);
      background-repeat: no-repeat;

      Width Height
      background-size: 200px 200px;
      background-size: 100% 200px;
      
      height: 100vh;
      background-size: 100%;

      Cover the Box with Aspect Ratio
      background-size: cover;
    }

Background Attachment

To handle this behavior, we can use the background-attachment property. 

body{
      background: url(NCS.png);
      background-repeat: no-repeat;      
      height: 300vh;
      background-size: cover;

      Background is fixed relative to the visible viewport
      background-attachment: fixed;
    }

By using background-attachment: fixed, we can also create a parallax scrolling effect.
This effect is achieved by creating multiple containers, each with a height of 100vh, assigning different background images to each container, and applying background-attachment: fixed. When you scroll the page, the content moves while the background images remain fixed, creating the parallax effect.

CSS Sprites

CSS Sprites are an optimization technique used to reduce the number of HTTP requests made by a web page.

For example, you can download icons from flaticon.com and insert them into your webpage. If you download 10 icons and insert them one by one using the <img> element in HTML, they may appear side by side on the page. However, in a real project, icons are usually placed in different sections of the website.

After adding these images, open the browser Developer Tools, go to the Network tab, disable the cache, and press Ctrl + R to reload the page. Then filter by Images. You will notice that the browser sends multiple HTTP requests to the server one request for each image.

Now imagine a large-scale website with millions of images and millions of active users. This would result in millions of HTTP requests, creating a significant server overhead and negatively affecting performance.

This is where CSS Sprites become useful.

With CSS Sprites, we combine multiple small images into a single image file. The browser then downloads this file using only one HTTP request, and different parts of the image are displayed using CSS.

Creating a CSS Sprite

To create a sprite, you can use tools such as cssspritestool.com or any CSS Sprite Generator.

  1. Drag and drop all the icons you want to combine into the tool.
  2. Explore the available settings.
  3. Set the CSS class prefix to bg-.
  4. Generate the sprite and download the ZIP file.

After downloading, the ZIP file will contain:

  • One combined image file (the sprite image)
  • A CSS file (e.g., css-sprites.css) containing multiple classes

Each class represents one icon, and because we used the bg- prefix, every class name will start with bg-.

Using CSS Sprites in Your Project

To use an icon, simply apply the corresponding class to an element such as a <span> or <i>: 

<span class="bg-icon-name"></span>

These classes use background-image and background-position to display the correct portion of the sprite.

Once implemented, check the Network tab again in DevTools. You will see that only one image request is made for all icons.

Important Considerations

Do not overuse CSS Sprites. This technique should not be used for large images or images with different dimensions.

There are two main reasons:

  1. Combining large images results in a very large file, which defeats the purpose of optimization.
  2. CSS Sprites are not flexible if you update or replace a single image, you must regenerate the entire sprite.

For best results, use CSS Sprites only for small images, such as icons, logos, and UI elements.

Data URLs

Data URLs were formerly called Data URIs. They are an optimization technique used to reduce the number of HTTP requests.

With this technique, we can embed an image directly into an HTML document or a CSS stylesheet instead of loading it as a separate file.

For a demo, visit cssportal.com/image-to-data/, or simply search for a Data URI generator or Data URL generator.

Drag and drop an image into the tool. As soon as you drop the image, you will see a long sequence of characters generated in the Data URI box on the same page.

You can copy this sequence and paste it directly into your HTML or CSS file to display the image without making an additional HTTP request. 

<img src="NCS.png" alt="NCS logo image in png format">
Converted to URI now copy and paste the src value you will see an image
<img alt="NCS.png" All the URLs we discussed start with HTTP or HTTPS, which are protocols used to transfer data over the web. Data is another protocol that is used to represent a binary file as a sequence of characters.

In web developer tools, you can see data URLs in the Network tab. A data URL comes directly from memory, so the browser does not send a separate HTTP request to download the image. This happens because the image is embedded directly into the HTML document. As a result, the loading time shows as 0 ms.

Data URLs are used to reduce the number of HTTP requests, which can potentially improve loading performance. However, this approach has several drawbacks.

First, the size of a data URL is larger than the size of the original resource. If you use a tool that converts images into data URLs, you will notice both the original size and the new (larger) size. You can also paste the data URL into a text editor like Notepad to see how large it becomes.

Second, embedding too many images increases the size of your HTML or CSS files, making them heavier than their original size. This negatively affects performance.

Third, data URLs make maintenance more difficult. If you want to change an image in the future, you must regenerate the data URL and replace it manually. This workflow is inconvenient compared to simply replacing an image file in the file system.

Additionally, on mobile devices, data URLs are often slower to display than externally loaded images.

For these reasons, data URLs should be used sparingly, such as for a single small image like a website logo, but not for all images. Many websites have used this technique, but it is not recommended as a general solution.

Clipping

In CSS, we can easily clip or cut parts of an image. Just go to your browser and search for a CSS clip-path generator you’ll find many useful tools. With these tools, you can convert your image into different shapes. You simply choose a shape and copy the generated CSS code.

In the past, shape creation was mostly done using editing software like Photoshop or Illustrator. However, nowadays CSS has become very powerful and can handle many tasks on its own. This is one great example of how CSS can be used creatively. 

<img src="NCS.png" alt="" class="img-to-star">

Converting the image into a shape:
.img-to-star{
          clip-path: polygon(50% 0%, 61% 35%, 98% 35%, 68% 57%, 79% 91%, 50% 70%, 21% 91%, 32% 57%, 2% 35%, 39% 35%);
}

Filters

In CSS, we can also apply filters to images. There are many filter functions available in CSS. The VS Code snippets can help you quickly find them, such as grayscale, blur, contrast, brightness, saturate, drop-shadow, and more. These are functions used with the filter property. You can combine these filters with the :hover selector to create visual effects when hovering over an image. 

<img src="NCS.png" alt="" class="ncs-logo">

.ncs-logo{
        filter: grayscale(70%) blur(30px);
}

Background Image in Text
div {
          font-family: Impact, Haettenschweiler, 'Arial Narrow Bold', sans-serif;
          display: flex;
          justify-content: center;
          align-items: center;

          font-size: 3rem;
          font-weight: bold;

          background-image: url("your_image_name.png");
          background-size: cover;
          background-position: center;

          color: transparent;
          -webkit-background-clip: text;
          background-clip: text;
          -webkit-text-fill-color: transparent;

          width: 30rem;
          height: 10rem;
}

Always check new properties in caniuse before using it More About BG Clip

Supporting High=Density Screens

These days, many companies are creating high-density displays that contain more pixels. In the past, for example, the iPhone 3 had a resolution of 480 × 320. Later, in 2010, Apple launched the iPhone 4 with the same physical screen size but double the number of pixels, giving it a resolution of 960 × 640.

This was the first generation of high-density screens, which Apple called the Retina Display.

Now, let’s say we have an image that is 320 × 320 pixels. If we display this image on an iPhone 3, it will look perfectly fine. But what happens if we show the same image on an iPhone 4?

You might think it will appear the same size (320 × 320), but that’s not correct. A Retina display uses a scale factor of 2×, meaning each logical pixel is made up of 2 × 2 physical pixels. As a result, the image will still take up the same logical width on the screen, but it will be scaled up, and since raster images lose quality when scaled, the image will appear blurry.

To display the image sharply on Retina or high-density displays, we need to create another version of the image that is twice as large, for example 640 × 640 pixels.

Modern devices have two important types of resolution:

  • Physical resolution: The actual number of pixels on the device.
    Example: iPhone 4 → 960 × 640
  • Logical resolution: How the device behaves in terms of layout and CSS.
    Example: iPhone 4 → 480 × 320

In simple terms, CSS always works with logical resolution, not physical resolution.

Devices also have a Device Pixel Ratio (DPR). DPR is the ratio between physical pixels and logical pixels.
For example, the iPhone 4 has a DPR of 2.

Different screens have different DPR values. High-density screens always have a DPR greater than 1, which means they contain more physical pixels than logical pixels.

Now, let’s take a high-resolution image and convert it into different sizes. You can do this using Photoshop or any online tool. Open the image in Photoshop, go to the Image menu, and select Image Size. You’ll see the current dimensions of the image.

Start with a large version, for example 1200 × 1200, then go to File → Save As and create smaller versions, such as 800 × 800 for 2× screens.

When creating multiple image sizes, always start with the largest image and scale down to preserve image quality. 

<img src="base_image.jpg" alt=""
        srcset="img@1x.jpg 1x,
                img@2x.jpg 2x,
                img@3x.jpg 3x"
>

To provide this image for high-density screens, we are going to use the srcset attribute.
Using srcset, we can define a set of image sources for different screen densities.

For example, img.jpg 1x means that if the device pixel ratio is 1 (such as a standard display), the browser will use this image.
For devices with higher pixel ratios, we can provide different images with higher resolutions.

Multiple images are specified using comma-separated values, where each image is associated with its corresponding screen density.

Now, let’s apply CSS styling to the image. 

img{
        width: 400px;
}

Open the browser’s Developer Tools and go to the Network tab. You can see which image is being loaded by the browser.
If we don’t use srcset, the browser will load the same single image for all types of displays, regardless of screen size or resolution.

You should not use srcset for every image. It is meant only for specific images, such as hero images or important visuals, where you want the homepage to look beautiful and remain sharp and high-quality on all devices.

Resolution Switching

Sometimes we provide different images for mobile phones, tablets, and desktop devices. This is called the resolution switching problem.

To handle this scenario, we use different image resolutions for different devices. As discussed earlier, we often provide multiple versions of the same image for standard displays and high-density displays.

High-density (Retina) screens have more pixels packed into the same physical space, so they require higher-resolution images. For example, Apple devices have different screen resolutions:

  • iPhone 8: 375 × 667
  • iPad Pro: 768 × 1024
  • MacBook Pro 15: 1440 × 900

Since these devices have significantly different resolutions and pixel densities, serving a single low-resolution image to all devices can reduce image quality. Therefore, we provide higher-resolution images such as img@2x.jpg (or multiple image sizes) so each device can display the image clearly and efficiently. 

<img src="base_image.jpg" alt=""
        srcset="img@1x.jpg 1x,
                img@2x.jpg 2x,
                img@3x.jpg 3x"
>

Using Relative unit for sizing
img{
        width: 100vw;
}

After using relative units, the browser will resize the image to 100% of the viewport width on different devices. This operation is handled by the browser, but the more scaling the browser has to do, the more expensive it becomes, because the browser still needs to download the entire image into memory first.

This is where resolution switching helps. Instead of loading one large image for all devices, we can provide different versions of the same image for different screen sizes. We don’t need to create images for every single device. Usually, providing three versions small, medium, and large is enough.

When multiple image sizes are provided, the browser automatically selects the most appropriate image based on the device’s screen resolution and pixel density. In the earlier example, we used values like 1x, 2x, etc. Now, instead of pixel density, we use the actual width of the image by using the w unit, which represents the real width of each image.

With this approach, the browser understands the available image sizes and intelligently picks the best image according to the screen resolution and device pixel ratio, resulting in better performance and optimized loading. 

<img src="base_image.jpg" 
        srcset="img@1x.jpg 400w,
                img@2x.jpg 800w,
                img@3x.jpg 1200w"
>

If we want an image to take the full width of the screen on a mobile device, half the width on a tablet, and one-fourth of the screen on a desktop, we can achieve this using the sizes attribute of the <img> element. First, remove the 100vw property from the image. Then, use the sizes attribute to define how much space the image should occupy depending on the screen size.

The numbers used in sizes are somewhat arbitrary but should be based on the design requirements. When you resize the browser window, you will see the difference in the image displayed. By using the following code, we allow the browser to select the most appropriate image for the webpage or device.

  • srcset defines the available image sources with their sizes.
  • sizes specifies the final display width of the image in different scenarios: full width on mobile, half width on tablets, and one-fourth width on desktops. ⁉️Never Use 1x, 2x while using Sizes.
<img src="base_image.jpg" alt="image description here."
        srcset="img@1x.jpg 400w,
                img@2x.jpg 800w,
                img@3x.jpg 1200w"
        sizes="(max-width: 500px) 100vw,
               (max-width: 700px) 50vw,
               33vw"
>

There is no need to display the same units or values for images, as shown in the code above. Instead, you can use a tool called responsivebreakpoints.com . Play with the size steps, which are used to resize images and create size variations between them. According to best practices, you can set the maximum number of image variations to 5.

After uploading your file to the tool, you will see the generated breakpoints. You can then download the images as a ZIP or view the HTML5 <img> tag example, from which you can copy the code directly and paste it into your markup.

Using Modern Image Formats

Earlier, we learned about common raster image formats like JPEG, GIF, and PNG. WebP is a modern format that many developers are not yet familiar with. In this section, we are going to learn how to use modern image formats on a webpage.

Converting Img to webp

You might be wondering how to convert images to the WebP format. There are several ways to do this:

  • Photoshop: You can use a plugin called WebPShop to save images in WebP format.
  • Sketch: You can easily export images in any format you want, including WebP.
  • Online tools: Websites like CloudConvert allow you to convert images online. Simply upload your image, choose WebP as the output format, and click Convert. Take some time (around 20 minutes) to explore the tool and learn its features. After conversion, you can download the compressed WebP image.
  • Command-line tools: These can be used to automate image format conversions for multiple files efficiently.

When using WebP images on a website, you can insert them using the <img> element. It’s also a good practice to include the original JPEG version as a fallback, because some older browsers like Internet Explorer do not support WebP. If you want to serve the modern WebP format while maintaining compatibility, you can use the <picture> element with multiple source formats.

<picture>
  <source type="image/webp" srcset="image_name.webp">
</picture>

We can supply multiple sources to the <picture> element and provide different sizes for various screens. It really depends on the problem we need to solve. Always consider the problem first, and then write the code accordingly. 

<picture>
  <source type="image/webp" srcset="image_name.webp 1x, image_name.webp 2x">
</picture>

Use multiple sources in the <picture> element so that if one format doesn’t work, the browser will try the next. Always include an <img> element inside the <picture> element because it provides the alt attribute, which is used to give alternative text. Additionally, the <img> element acts as a fallback in case the <picture> element or its <source> elements are not supported by the browser. 

<picture>
  <source type="image/webp" srcset="image_name.webp">
  <source type="image/webp" srcset="image_name.jpg">
  <img src="image_name.jpg" alt="image description here.">
</picture>

Art Direction

Sometimes, we want to display a zoomed-in or cropped version of an image for different screen sizes. This is called art direction. For example, if you visit the earls.ca website and resize the browser, you’ll notice that the image appears differently depending on the screen size.

The idea is to show different variants of an image on different devices for instance, a zoomed-in version or a cropped version. To achieve this, you can create two versions of the same image: one larger version (e.g., 2x or 1600px) and one cropped version. Then, using the <source> element with the media attribute, you can serve the appropriate image variant depending on the screen size. 

<picture>
  <source media="(max-width: 500px)" srcset="image_nam_cropped.jpg">
  <source media="(min-width: 501px)" srcset="image_name.jpg">
  <img src="image_name.jpg" alt="here the image description.">
</picture>

Scalable Vector Graphics

We have already covered the formats of SVGs, which are vector images. These graphics are defined by mathematical vectors, so they always remain sharp at any size. SVG images are commonly used for logos, icons, and simple graphics. You can download icons from websites like SVGRepo and resize them on a website notice that they stay perfectly sharp. For backgrounds, you can visit SVG Backgrounds, test the images there, and download them for use. Since SVGs are mathematically defined, they scale without losing quality. Additionally, SVG images are supported by over 99.4% of browsers. Always prefer SVGs for graphics and backgrounds.

Font Icons

Another way to display icons on your website is by using icon fonts. Icon fonts are special fonts where each character represents an icon instead of a regular letter, like “abcd” in a normal font. There are many icon font libraries available, but the most popular ones are Font Awesome, Ionicons, and Material Design Icons.

For example, to use Font Awesome:

  1. Visit the Font Awesome website, where you’ll find both free and pro versions.
  2. Click “Start” and use your email to create a kit. A kit is basically an ID that contains your selected icons. You can rename the kit to something meaningful for your project.
  3. In the kit settings, you can choose the technology you want to use web fonts (most common) or SVGs (which offer more control).
  4. There is a section called “How to Use” where you will find the code or script for your kit. Add this to your HTML document.
  5. Browse the icons in your kit and select the ones you need (free icons if you’re using the free plan).
  6. Once you select an icon, you can customize it as needed. Click “Start Using This Icon” to copy the generated HTML code. By default, the code uses an <i> tag, but you can change it to a <span> if you prefer.

The icon code contains two classes:

  • fas – Font Awesome Solid
  • fa-[icon-name] – identifies the specific icon

In the future, you only need to change the fa-[icon-name] class to display a different icon. You can also resize or style icons using additional Font Awesome classes, such as fa-2x to double the icon size. To further style the icon with CSS, wrap it in a <span> element and apply your styles. 

<span class="bell-icon">
  <i class="fas fa-bell fa-2x"></i>
</span>
